Deciding equivalence with sums and the empty type Conference: CONF ’yy Month d–d, 20yy, City, ST, Country


Abstract
The logical technique of focusing can be applied to the λ \lambda -calculus; in a simple type system with atomic types and negative type formers (functions, products, the unit type), its normal forms coincide with β ​ η \beta\eta -normal forms. Introducing a saturation phase gives a notion of quasi-normal forms in presence of positive types (sum types and the empty type). This rich structure let us prove the decidability of β ​ η \beta\eta -equivalence in presence of the empty type, the fact that it coincides with contextual equivalence, and a finite model property.

1
Introduction

1.1
Notion of equivalences

For a given type system, there may be several notions of program
equivalence of interest.
One may define a notion of syntactic equivalence by a system of
equations between terms, such as
\beta
-equivalence and

\eta
-equivalence, or their union
{\beta\eta}
-equivalence.
A more extensional notion of equivalence is contextual or
observational equivalence, which checks that the two terms behave in
the same way under all contexts.
Finally, a semantic notion of equivalence is given by interpreting
terms in a certain mathematical space, typically morphisms in
categories with a certain structure, and considering two terms
equivalent if they are equal under all interpretations.
One can generally prove that considering all interpretations in the
category of sets suffices to distinguish two observably distinct
terms, and certain type systems have the stronger
finite model

property that considering all interpretations in
finite
sets
suffices to distinguish observably distinct terms.

Contextual equivalence has a clear, compact definition, it is the
notion of equivalence that corresponds to programmers’ intuition, but
it is difficult to prove and reason about.
Syntactic equivalence can be used as part of syntactic typing
judgments, and may be easier to prove decidable.
Semantic models provide a more abstract point of view on the identity
of programs, and may enable the powerful method of
normalization-by-evaluation.

For the untyped
\lambda
-calculus,
Böhm 1968
proved that

{\beta\eta}
-equivalence and observational equivalence coincide.
Untyped
\beta
-reduction is not normalizing, so equivalence is undecidable.

For the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus with atomic types, functions
and pairs – what we call the
negative
fragment – we also know
that these notions of equivalence coincide.
Furthermore, typed equivalence is decidable: we can define and compute

\beta
-short
\eta
-long normal forms, and two terms are

{\beta\eta}
-equivalent exactly when they have the same normal form.

Friedman 1975
proved that two terms equal in all set-theoretic
models are equivalent, and
Statman 1982
sharpened the result by
proving that finite sets suffice to distinguish inequivalent terms – the
finite model property.

This pleasant setting is quickly eroded by moving to richer
programming languages and type systems.
Adding notions of side-effects, for example, makes the general

\beta\eta
-equivalence unsound.
Even in the realm of pure, total programming languages, adding
parametric polymorphism (System F and beyond) makes

\beta\eta
-equivalence strictly weaker than contextual equivalence.

1.2
Sums and empty type

An interesting middle-ground is the
full
simply-typed

\lambda
-calculus, with not only atomic types, functions, pairs and
the unit type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}
but also sum types and the empty type

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
.
There, results on program equivalence have been surprisingly long to
come, because of deep difficulties caused by mixing functions
(negative connectives) and sums (positive connectives), and the strong
behavior of the empty type: in an inconsistent context, all terms are
equal.

The first decidability result for the system with non-empty sums was

Ghani 1995
, using advanced rewriting techniques later
simplified by
Lindley 2007
.
It was followed by normalization-by-evaluation
results
[
Altenkirch et al. 2001
;
Balat et al. 2004
]
that also established
decidability of equivalence in the non-empty case using the
categorical structure introduced in
Fiore and Simpson 1999
.
Decidability in the presence of the empty type is still open – we
propose a proof.

Finally, the only known completeness result for set-theoretic models
is
Dougherty and Subrahmanyam 2000
; it only holds for non-empty sums, and
relies in an essential way on infinite sets.
The finite model property is only conjectured – we propose a proof,
including in presence of the empty type.

1.3
Focusing

Focusing
[
Andreoli 1992
]
is a general technique that uses the
notion of invertibility of inference rules to define a
focused
subset of any
logic that is complete but removes some redundant proofs.

A recent branch of work on
maximal
multi-focusing

[
Chaudhuri et al. 2008a
;
Chaudhuri et al. 2012
]

has demonstrated that focused proofs can be further restricted to
become even more canonical: in each application to a specific logic,
the resulting representations are equivalent to existing representations
capturing the identity of proofs – proof nets for linear logic,
expansion proofs for classical logic.

Scherer and Rémy 2015
applied focusing to the
\lambda
-calculus with
non-empty sums. Completeness of focusing there means that any term has
a
{\beta\eta}
-equivalent focused term. Their counterpart of maximal
multi-focusing is
saturation
: saturated terms of the focused

\lambda
-calculus introduce and deconstruct neutral terms of sum type
as early as possible – when they are
new
, they were not
derivable in the previous saturation phase. Canonicity of this
representation gives yet another decision procedure for

{\beta\eta}
-equivalence of
\lambda
-terms with non-empty sums.

The present work extends saturated focusing to the full simply-typed
lambda calculus, with units and in particular the empty type. The
suitably extended notion of saturated form retains its canonicity
properties in the full system. This means that
\beta\eta
-equivalence
with empty types is decidable – by converting terms to their
saturated focused form. From two distinct saturated forms, one can
furthermore build a well-typed context that distinguishes them. This
proves that contextual equivalence implies equality of normal forms,
and thus
{\beta\eta}
-equivalence: any term is
{\beta\eta}
-equivalent to
its normal form, so two terms with the same normal form are

{\beta\eta}
-equivalent. Our distinguishing context needs only
instantiate atomic types with finite sets, giving a finite model
property.

Extending saturated focusing to the empty type requires adding
a requirement that saturation phases be complete for provability: not
only must they introduce all new neutrals of positive type for use in
the rest of the term, but they must at least introduce one such
neutral for each type deducible in the current context. As
a consequence, we can prove a Saturation Consistency theorem which is
key to our canonicity: if two saturated terms are distinct, then their
context must be consistent.

1.4
Contributions

We establish the following results in the simply-typed

\lambda
-calculus with atoms, functions, pairs, the unit type, sums
and the empty type
{\mathsf{\Lambda{}C}{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to}}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}})}}
:

•

Saturated terms provide a notion of
quasi
-normal form;
equivalent quasi-normal forms are not necessarily

\alpha
-equivalent, but are related by a local, easily decidable
relation of
invertible commutation conversions

(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}})
.

•

{\beta\eta}
-equivalence is decidable.

•

{\beta\eta}
-equivalence and contextual equivalence coincide,
along with set-theoretic equivalence in all models where atomic
types are interpreted by closed types.

•

The finite model property holds – as closed types in this
system have finitely many inhabitants.

Our notion of
{\beta\eta}
-equivalence is the
strong

equivalence on sums. It corresponds to equality of morphisms in the
free bi-cartesian closed category – see
Scherer 2016
,
Section 3.2.2.

1.5
Plan

Section

2
(
2
Equivalences in the full
\lambda
-calculus
)
introduces the full
\lambda
-calculus we
shall consider in this work, along with the various notions of
equivalence (
{\beta\eta}
, contextual, set-theoretic) we will
discuss. We prove some elementary results:
{\beta\eta}
-equivalence
implies contextual equivalence in all closed models, which coincides
with set-theoretic equivalence in all closed models.

Section

3
(
3
Focusing
)
presents focusing, before detailing the
focused
\lambda
-calculus extended with the unit type and empty
type. We formulate the computational counterpart of the completeness
theorem: any
\lambda
-term has a
\beta\eta
-equivalent focused term.

Section

4
(
4
Saturated focused
\lambda
-calculus
)
presents the saturated subset of the
focused
\lambda
-calculus as defined by a different system of
inference rules. Compared to the simpler system of

Scherer and Rémy 2015
, this saturated system is parametrized over
a
selection function
that selects the neutrals to split over
during saturation. The saturated system is, again, computationally
complete with respect to focused terms.

Section

5
(
5
Saturation consistency
)
establishes the main
meta-theoretic property of saturated terms in presence of the empty
type, namely that saturating an inconsistent context will always find
a proof of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
. In other words, if two saturated terms differ
after a saturation phase instead of both ending on an absurdity
elimination, we know that they differ in consistent contexts. This
result is key to extending the distinguishability result of

Dougherty and Subrahmanyam 2000
to a system with empty types.

Finally,
Section

6
(
6
Canonicity
)
establishes the central result of
this work: if two saturated
\lambda
-terms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
are
syntactically distinct (modulo invertible commuting conversions), then
there exists a closed type model
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
in which we have
a distinguishing context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}\right]}}
such

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}})}}\right]}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}})}}\right]}}
are
closed booleans (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}
), one of them equal to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{true}}}

and the other to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{false}}}
. By contraposition, this proves that two
terms contextually equivalent in all models have the same saturated
normal forms – giving decidability – and in particular are

{\beta\eta}
-equivalent.

For lack of space, our statements do not come with their proofs, but
proof outlines are given in
Appendix

B
(
Appendix B
Proof outlines
)
.

Sections 1 to 5, in particular the presentations of focusing
and saturated normal forms, correspond to content that was
presented in the thesis manuscript
Scherer 2016
. In the
present article, definitions and proofs in these sections are
given with minimal amount of details for economy of space; they
are presented in full in the manuscript. In contrast, Section 6,
which presents the key canonicity result enabling the
contributions of this article, is entirely new, and presented in
more detail.

2
Equivalences in the full
\lambda
-calculus

2.1
Typing rules and
{\beta\eta}
-equivalence

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}\right)}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright_{\beta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\par{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A_{1}\times A_{2}}})}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright_{\eta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{1}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{2}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\right)}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright_{\beta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}/}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}\par{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A_{1}+A_{2}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}/}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright_{\eta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}/}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}\\
\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright_{\eta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:A}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}/}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright_{\eta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{absurd}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}})}}}}:A}}}\\
\text{Derived rules:}\par\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\eta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\eta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}}}:A}}}

Figure

1
gives the grammar and typing rules for the
full simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus
{\mathsf{\Lambda{}C}{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to}}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}})}}
. By symmetry with the
pair projections
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}
, we use
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}
for sum
injection. We use
\left(\dots\right)^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i\in I}}}}
for a family of objects indexed by

{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i\in I}}}
. The common indexing family, when dealing with syntactic binary
operations, is
\{1,2\}
, and we will most of the time leave it
implicit. Finally,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}
is a compact syntax for
our full sum-elimination syntax,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}~\mathtt{with}~}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}_{1}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{2}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}_{2}}}\right)}}
.

Definition 1
.

A
closed
type does not contain atomic types.
1
1

1

A
reviewer remarks that the terminology “atomic type” is
awkward; if “atom” means “indecomposable”, then unit types

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
could arguably be considered atomic. In
retrospect, we agree that “type variable”, as naturally used
in polymorphic type systems, would be a better terminology, and
plan to use it in the future.

Definition 2
.

We write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}A}}
when
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
exists such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:A}}
.

We define
{\beta\eta}
-equivalence as the smallest congruent relation

(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}})
closed by the union of the
\beta
-reduction
relation
(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright_{\beta}}}}})
and the
\eta
-expansion relation

(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright_{\eta}}}}})
of
Figure

2
. We have
not explicited the full typing assumptions, but only type-preserving
rewrites are considered. The derived rules are not primitives, they
are derivable from
\eta
-expansion at unit and empty type.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}
are of type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}
, then they both rewrite to

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}
and are thus equal. The derivation for equality under
absurdity is less direct: if the current typing context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}
is
inconsistent, that is there is a derivation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}})}}
,
then any term of
any
type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:A)}}
can be seen as the
result of the substitution
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}/}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}
for a variable
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}

that does not appear in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
, and is thus equal to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{absurd}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}})}}
;
in particular, any two terms of the same type are equal.

Theorem 1

(Strong normalization)
.

\beta
-reduction is strongly normalizing in the full simply-typed

\lambda
-calculus
{\mathsf{\Lambda{}C}{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to}}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}})}}
.

Theorem 2

(Confluence)
.

\beta
-reduction is confluent for the full simply-typed

\lambda
-calculus
{\mathsf{\Lambda{}C}{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to}}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}})}}
: each term has a unique
\beta
-short
normal form.

Lemma 1

(Inversion)
.

A closed
\beta
-normal form (in an empty context) starts with an
introduction form.

2.2
Contextual equivalence

A common definition of contextual equivalence, for System F for
example, is that two terms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathpunct{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}:A}}
are
contextually equivalent if there exists no separating context

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}:A}}}}\right]}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\right]}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\beta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}\right]}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\beta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}
. For a trivial example, if

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}})

, then

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}\right]}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}}}}})}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}})}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}})}}}}

separates
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
.

This definition is too weak in presence of atomic types. Consider the
context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}})
and the terms

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\mathpunct{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}}
. We want a definition of
contextual equivalence that declares these terms inequivalent, but
there is no distinguishing context in the sense above as we have no
information on
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
, and thus no way to provide distinct values for

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
. The variables
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}

could
be
distinct depending on what is the unknown type represented by the
abstract type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
.

Definition 3
.

A
model

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
is a mapping from atomic types to closed
types.

If
x
is some syntactic object containing types, we write

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}(x)
for the result of replacing each atomic type in
x
by
its image in the model
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
. If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:A}}
holds, then it
is also the case that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}(A)}}
holds; we may write
the term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}})
as well, to emphasize that we look at its
typing in the model.

Definition 4
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathpunct{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}:A}}
and for a given model
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
, we say
that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
are
contextually equivalent in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
,
written
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{ctx}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}})}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
, if

\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}},\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}(A)}}}}\right]}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}})}}\right]}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\beta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}})}}\right]}}

We say that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
are
contextually equivalent
,
written
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
, if they are contextually equivalent
in all models.

2.3
Semantic equivalence

Definition 5

(Semantics of types)
.

For a closed type
A
we define the set of
semantic values

of
A
, written
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}
, by induction on
A
as
follows:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad\eqdef\quad}l}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\hfil\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}&\text{total functions from ${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}$ to ${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{B}\,\right\rrbracket}}$}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\times B}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\hfil\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}&\{(v,w)\mid v\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}},w\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{B}\,\right\rrbracket}}\}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\hfil\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}&\{\star\}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A+B}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\hfil\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}&\{(1,v)\mid v\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}\}\uplus\{(2,w)\mid w\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{B}\,\right\rrbracket}}\}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\hfil\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}&\emptyset\\
\end{array}

For an arbitrary type
A
we write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
for

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}(A)}\,\right\rrbracket}}
.

We remark that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
is always a finite type, whose elements
can be enumerated. It is thus decidable whether two elements of

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
are equal as mathematical objects; at function
types, one compares two functions pointwise on their finite domain.

Definition 6

(Semantics of environments)
.

For a closed typing environment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}
, we define the set

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}
of
semantic valuations
,
functions from the domain of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}
to semantic values such that:

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\par\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\par\{G\ \mid\ \forall{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:A}}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}},\quad G({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}\}

We write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}})}\,\right\rrbracket}}
.

Definition 7

(Semantics of typing judgments)
.

We write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}A}\,\right\rrbracket}}
for the set of functions from
semantic valuations of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}
to semantic values in
A
:

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}A}\,\right\rrbracket}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}

.
We write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}A}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
for

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}(A)}\,\right\rrbracket}}
.

Definition 8

(Semantics of terms)
.

For a term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:A}}
in a judgment with closed types, we
write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}
for the set-theoretic semantics of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
,
as an object of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}A}\,\right\rrbracket}}
. The (natural) definition
is given in full in
Definition
(
Definition

(
)
.
)
, but for example

\begin{array}[]{l@{\eqdef}l}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}(G)\hfil\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}&(v\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}(G,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\mapsto v))\end{array}

We write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}})}\,\right\rrbracket}}
.

Definition 9

(Semantic equivalence)
.

For any terms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathpunct{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}:A}}
and model
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
, we say
that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
are
semantically equivalent in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
,
written
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{sem}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}})}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
, if their semantics are
(pointwise) equal.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{sem}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}})}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\ (\forall G\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}},\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}(G)={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}(G)\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}})

We say that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
are
semantically equivalent
,
written
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{sem}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
, if they are semantically equivalent
in all models
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
.

2.4
Easy relations between equivalences

Theorem 3

(
{\beta\eta}
is semantically sound)
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{sem}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
.

Theorem 4

(Semantic equivalence implies contextual equivalence)
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{sem}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
.

On models using closed types
(Long version)

Our definition of a model
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
allows to instantiate atomic types
with any closed type; this instantiation happens in the world of
syntax, before we interpret types as sets. It is more common, when
giving set-theoretic semantics, to instantiate atoms only in the
semantics, defining models as mapping from atomic types to
arbitrary
sets
.

We are fortunate that our grammar of closed types is expressive
enough to describe any finite set; if we did not have units
(
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
), for example, we could not do this. Our
more syntactic notion of model can be shared by our definition of
contextual and semantic equivalence, which is very pleasing.

Note that, as a consequence, the finite model property is sort of
built into our definition of the semantic and contextual
equivalences: we may only distinguish atoms by instantiating them
with
finite
sets, not arbitrary sets. One may wonder, then,
whether a notion of semantic equivalence that allows to
instantiate atoms with
infinite
sets would behave
differently – did we really prove the finite model property, or
just enforce it by stifling our notion of equivalence?

The coincidence of the finite and infinite interpretations is
a consequence of the later results of this work on the coincidence
of semantic and
\beta
-equivalence. If we added the ability to
instantiate atoms by infinite sets, we would distinguish more: we
could not prove more terms equal, but would only prove more terms

different
. But any pair of terms equal in the finite-set
semantics is
{\beta\eta}
-equivalent, and

Theorem

3
(
Theorem 3

(
{\beta\eta}
is semantically sound)
.
)
seamlessly extends to infinite
sets – those terms must still be equal in an infinite-set
semantics.

2.5
Fun-less types and reification

To prove that contextual equivalence implies semantic
equivalence, we build a reification procedure
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}(v)}}
that goes
from
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
to closed terms in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}(A)
and
satisfies the following properties:

\forall v,\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}(v)}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}=v\par\forall({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:A}}),\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}})}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}

This is difficult in the general case, as it corresponds to
a normalization-by-evaluation procedure – when you can furthermore
prove that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}(v)}}
is always a normal term. The reification
of finite sums and products is straightforward, but function types are
delicate; intuitively, to reify a function one builds a decision tree
on its input, which requires an enumeration procedure for the input
type
[
Altenkirch and Uustalu 2004
]
which may itself be a function, etc.

In the present case, however, the fact that we work with closed types
(no atoms) enables a useful hack: we can eliminate function types by
rewriting them in isomorphic types expressed in

{\mathsf{\Lambda{}C}{(\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}})}}
only. This was inspired by an idea of
Danko Ilik (see for example
Ilik 2015
), which removes sum types
rather than function types. In presence of atomic or infinite types,
neither sum nor function types can be fully removed. In absence of
atoms, function types can be fully removed, but sum types cannot –
there is no type isomorphic to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}
in
{\mathsf{\Lambda{}C}{(\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to}}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}})}}
.

Figure

3
(
3Fun-less data types
)
defines the fun-less
\lfloor{A}\rfloor
for each
closed type
A
. Its definition is structurally recursive, and uses
an auxiliary definition
\llfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}}\rrfloor
that takes
a function type whose left-hand side
A
is fun-less, and is
structurally recursive on this left-hand side.
We also define pair of transformations
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\rfloor_{A}}}
from
A

to
\lfloor{A}\rfloor
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lceil{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\rceil_{A}}}
from
\lfloor{A}\rfloor
to
A
,
on both terms and semantic values. On semantic values they are
inverse; on terms they are inverse modulo
{\beta\eta}
-equivalence. The
(natural) definitions are given in full in
Definition
(
Definition

(
)
.
)
. It uses auxiliary
definitions
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\rrfloor_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}}}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llceil{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\rrceil_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}}}}
, and has for example

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lfloor{v}\rfloor_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llfloor{w\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lfloor{v({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lceil{w}\rceil_{A}}})}\rfloor_{B}}}}\rrfloor_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}}}}

and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\rrfloor_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A_{1}\times A_{2}}}\to B}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\llfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{2}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{2}\right)}}}}}}}}\rrfloor_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A_{2}\to B}}}}}}}}}\rrfloor_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A_{1}\to({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A_{2}\to B}})}}}}}
.

Finally, we also have that the isomorphisms on semantic values and
ground terms commute.

Theorem 5

(Reification)
.

For each semantic value
v
in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}
we can define
a closed term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}(v)}}
such that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}(v)}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}=v
.

Theorem

5
(
Theorem 5

(Reification)
.
)
establishes that semantic inhabitation and
provability coincide at closed types.

Corollary 1

(Inhabited or inconsistent)
.

For
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}},A
closed, if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}A}}
then either

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}A}}
or
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}
.

Lemma 2
.

For any closed term of closed type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:A}}

we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}})}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
.

Theorem 6

(Contextual equivalence implies semantic equivalence)
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{sem}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
.

3
Focusing

Consider the usual description of
\beta
-normal forms in the purely
negative fragment of the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus,

{\mathsf{\Lambda{}C}{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to}}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}})}}
.

\begin{array}[]{llll}\text{values}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:=}}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}\right)}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\\
\text{neutrals}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:=}}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{i}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\end{array}

Values, the story goes, are a sequence of
constructors
applied to
a neutral, which is a sequence of
destructors
applied to
a variable. It is even possible and easy to capture the set of

\beta
-short
\eta
-long normal forms by adding a typing restriction
to this grammar, asking for the first neutral term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
found in
a value to be of atomic type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}})}}
.

Unfortunately, adding sum types to this picture shatters it
irreparably. If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{i}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}}
is a constructor, it should go in
values, and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}~\mathtt{with}~\dots}}
is a destructor, it should be
a neutral term former. Adding
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{i}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}
to the grammar of values
seems innocuous, but adding
match
to neutrals raises
a question: should we ask the branches to be neutrals

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}
or values
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}
?
Neither choices work very well.

Asking branches to be neutrals means that the term

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}}}}\right)}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}}}}

is not a valid normal form, and in fact has no valid normal form! We
cannot force all constructors to occur outside branches, as in this
example we fundamentally need to choose a different constructor in
each branch – committing to either
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}}
or
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}}

before matching on
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
would make us stuck, unable to complete our
attempt with a well-formed term.

On the other hand, letting branches be any value introduces normal
forms that really should not be normal forms, such as

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{1}\;({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\right)}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}})}}
, clearly
equivalent, for any value of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
, to the neutral
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
.

The solution to this problem comes from logic. Logicians remark that
some inference rules are
invertible
and some are

non-invertible
. A rule is invertible when, used during
goal-directed proof search, it preserves provability: if the
conclusion was provable (maybe using another rule), then applying this
rule results in premises that are also provable. For example, consider
the implication and disjunction introduction rules:

\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}},A\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}B}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}A_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A_{1}+A_{2}}}}

Implication introduction is invertible – this can be proved by
inverting the rule, showing a derivation of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}},A\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}B

with open premise
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}
. Disjunction
introduction is not: if one decides to prove, say,
A_{1}
, one may
get stuck while having chosen to prove
A_{2}
would have worked. Or
maybe one needs to delay this choice until some hypothesis of the
contexts is explored – which is the heart of our

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}}
example.

Andreoli’s focusing
[
Andreoli 1992
]
is a technique to restrict
a logic, make its proof term more canonical, by imposing additional
restrictions based on the invertibility of rules. One easy restriction
is that invertible rules, when they can be applied to a judgment,
should be applied as early as possible. The more interesting
restriction is that when one starts applying non-invertible rules,
focusing forces us to apply them as long as possible, as long as the
formula introduced in premises remain at a type where a non-invertible
rule exists. For a complete reference on focusing in intuitionistic
logic, see
Liang and Miller 2007
.

In programming terms, the fact that the right implication rule is
invertible corresponds to an
inversion principle
on values:
without loss of generality, one can consider that any value of type

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}
is of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}
. Any value of type

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A_{1}\times A_{2}}}
is of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}\right)}}
. This is strictly true
for closed values in the empty context, but it is true
modulo
equivalence
even in non-empty contexts, as is witnessed by the

\eta
-expansion principles. If a value
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}})}}
is
not a
\lambda
-abstraction, we can consider the equivalent term

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}}}
.

But it is
not
the case that any value of type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A+B}}
is
of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}
, as our example

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}}
demonstrated. Inspired by
focusing we look back at our grammar of
{\beta\eta}
-normal forms: it is
not about constructors and destructors, it is about term-formers that
correspond to invertible rules and those that do not. To gracefully
insert sums into this picture, the non-invertible
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}}

should go into the
neutrals
, and case-splitting should be
a
value
.
Scherer and Rémy 2015
introduce focusing in more
details, and present a grammar of
focused
normal forms is,
lightly rephrased, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll@{~}l}\text{values}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:=}}&~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}\right)}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}\\
&&&\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}\\
\text{choice terms}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:=}}&~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}})}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A+B}}}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}\\
\text{negative neutrals}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:=}}&~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{i}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\\
\text{positive neutrals}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:=}}&~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{i}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}})}}\end{array}

The type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
on the last line denotes a
negative
type,
defined as a type whose head connective has an invertible
right-introduction rule:
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}
or
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\times B}}
or

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}
. This means that if the argument of an injection

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}}
is itself of sum type, it must be of the form

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}}
as well; this enforces the focusing restriction that
non-invertible rules are applied as long as the type allows.

It is interesting to compare this grammar to bidirectional type
systems – when used to understand canonical forms rather than for
type inference. Focusing generalizes the idea that some parts of the
term structure (constructors) are canonically determined by type
information, while some parts (neutrals) are not. It generalizes
bidirectional typing by taking the typing environment into account as
well as the goal type (variables of sum type are split during the
inversion phase), and refines the application syntax
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}

into the sharper
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}
where both sub-terms have a neutral
spine.

Our work builds on this focused representation, easily extended with
an empty type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
. Our presentation of the type system is
different, farther away from the standard
\lambda
-calculi and closer
to recent presentation of focused systems, by using polarized syntax
for types with explicit shifts – it clarifies the structure of
focused systems. Instead of distinguishing positive and negative types
based on their head connectives, we define two disjoint syntactic
categories
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
, with explicit embeddings
\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\right\rangle^{+}
,

\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}
to go from one to the other. In particular, atoms are
split in two groups, the positive atoms of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
and the
negative atoms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}
– there is a global mapping from atoms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
to
polarities, a given atom is either always positive or always
negative. Sometimes we need to consider either types of a given
polarity or atoms of any polarity; we use
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
for positive types
or negative atoms, and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
for negative types of positive atoms.

We present this focused
\lambda
-calculus in

Figure

4
(
4Cut-free focused

λ

-terms
)
. The focusing discipline is
enforced by the inference rules which alternate between four different
judgments:

•

The
invertible
judgment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
corresponds to invertible
phases in focused proof search.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
is a typing environment
mapping variables to negative types
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
or positive atoms

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}
contains only positive types; it is the part of the
context that must be decomposed by invertible rules before the end
of the phase. The two positions
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
in
the goal are either formulas or empty
(\emptyset)
, and exactly one
of them is non-empty in any valid judgment. If the goal is
a negative formula
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
, it has yet to be introduced by invertible
rules during this phase; once it becomes atomic or positive it moves
to the other position
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
.

•

The
negative focus
judgment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}

corresponds to a non-invertible phase focused on a (negative)
formula in the context.

•

The
positive focus
judgment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}

corresponds to a non-invertible phase focused on a (positive)
formula in the goal.

•

The
choice-of-focusing
judgment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{foc}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
corresponds to the moment of the proof
search (reading from the conclusion to the premises) where the
invertible phase is finished, but no choice of focus has been made
yet. Focusing on the goal on the right uses a positive neutral to
prove a positive type –
foclc-concl-pos
. Focusing on the
left uses a negative neutral. If the neutral has a positive type, it
is
let
-bound to a variable and the proof continue with an
invertible phase –
foclc-let-pos
. If it has a negative
atomic type, then it must be equal to the goal type and the proof is
done –
foclc-concl-neg
.

Figure 4:
Cut-free focused
\lambda
-terms

\begin{array}[]{ll@{~}l@{~}l}\text{negative types}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M}}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:=}}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}^{-},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Z}}^{-}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{1}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{2}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\mid\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}\\

&&&\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{absurd}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}\\
\text{focusing terms}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}g}}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:=}}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}})}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\mid{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-})}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}\\
\text{negative neutrals}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:=}}&{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}})}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}\\
\text{positive neutrals}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}q}}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:=}}&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+})}}\end{array}\\
