9.24994pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:\emptyset\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{foc}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{foclc-let-pos}\par\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\par\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{1}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{2}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{i}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{i}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid\emptyset}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\right\rangle^{+}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{i}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{i}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{1}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{2}}}}\\
\text{shift-or-atom notations}\par\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\right\rangle^{+}_{\mathsf{a}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\right\rangle^{+}&\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}\right\rangle^{+}_{\mathsf{a}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}\\
\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}_{\mathsf{a}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}&\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}}\right\rangle^{-}_{\mathsf{a}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}\end{array}

The notation
\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\right\rangle^{+}_{\mathsf{a}}
takes a negative-or-atomic type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

and returns a positive type. It is used in the rule

foclc-inv-foc
that concludes an invertible phase and starts the
choice-of-focusing phase. It may only be applied when the positive
context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}
is of the form
\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle^{+}_{\mathsf{a}}
for some
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
,
that is, when it only contains negative or atomic formulas – it has
been fully decomposed.

Notice that the sum-elimination rule in the invertible judgment
eliminates a variable
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
, and not an arbitrary term, and
re-introduces variables with the same name
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
, shadowing the
previous hypothesis of sum type: there is no need to refer to it
anymore as we learned its value. This cute trick is not fundamental
for a focused calculus, but it corresponds to the intuition of
the corresponding sequent-calculus rule, and let us actually remove
positive types from the context to have a negative-or-atomic context
at the end of the phase.

For any judgment, for example
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
, we use the
version without a term position, for example
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
, as
the proposition that there exists a well-typed term:

\exists{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}},\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
. This is also an invitation
to think of the derivation as a logic proof rather than a typed
program.

Our focused terms are
cut-free
in the sense that they contain
no
\beta
-redexes, even modulo commuting conversions. The rule

foclc-let-pos
does look like a cut, proving

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{foc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
from
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}

and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:\emptyset\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
, but
notice that substituting the negative neutral
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
inside the
invertible proof
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
would not create a
\beta
-redex: we know that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
is matched over during the invertible phase, but
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
cannot
start with a constructor so
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}~\mathtt{with}~\dots}}
cannot reduce. If
you are interested in focused systems that do have cuts and
interesting dynamic semantics, then the abstract machine calculi of

Curien et al. 2016
are a better starting point.

3.1
(Non-)canonicity

When we look at the purely negative fragment of our calculus

{\mathsf{\Lambda{}C}{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to}}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}})}}
, we can prove that the focused

\lambda
-terms correspond exactly to the usual notion of

\beta
-short
\eta
-long normal forms. For example, consider the
valid terms for the judgment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}^{+}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Z}}^{-}_{1}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Z}}^{-}_{2}}}}}}};\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{?}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}^{+}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Z}}^{-}_{1}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Z}}^{-}_{2}}}}}\mid\emptyset

. Neither
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
nor
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}}}}
, that would be
well-typed for the corresponding un-focused judgment, are valid
according to our inference rules. There is exactly one valid
derivation in our system, for the term

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{1}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}})}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{2}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}})}}}}\right)}}}}}
which is the

\eta
-long normal form of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
at this type.

A consequence of this result is that the focused
\lambda
-calculus is
canonical for the purely negative fragment (or, in fact, the purely
positive fragment): if we have

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathtt{n}}}};\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathpunct{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}
with

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\neq_{\alpha}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
– these are known to be equivalent in the
negative fragment.

Focusing is not canonical anymore in mixed-polarity settings. The
first source of non-canonicity is that there may a free choice of
ordering of invertible rules in a phase; consider the judgment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{1}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{2}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{?}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}\mid\emptyset

for example, one may either do a case-split on
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{1}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{2}}}
or
introduce a
\lambda
-abstraction for the function type

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}
:
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{?}}}}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}
or

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{?}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}}}}
are both valid term
prefixes. This is solved by declaring that we do not care about the
ordering of invertible rules within a single phase.

Definition 10
.

We define the equivalence relation
(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}})
as allowing
well-typed permutations of two adjacent invertible rules. For
example we have

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{absurd}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{absurd}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})}}}}}
.

From now on any notion of normal form being discussed should be
understood as a
quasi
-normal form, a normal form modulo
invertible commuting conversions
(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}})
. This is
a reasonable approximation of the idea of normal form, as it is easily
decidable. Indeed, while in general commuting conversions may relate
very different terms, they can be easily decided inside a single
invertible phase, for example by imposing a fixed ordering on
invertible rules. By definition of invertibility, any ordering
preserves completeness.

The other more fundamental source of non-canonicity is that two
non-invertible phases may be independent from each other, and thus be
ordered in several possible ways, giving distinct but equivalent
terms. For example,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}_{1}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{2}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}_{2}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{2}\right)}}}}}}

and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{2}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}_{2}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}_{1}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{2}\right)}}}}}}

are equivalent at type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}^{-}}}
if

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1}\notin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}_{2},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{2}\notin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}_{1}
. This source of
redundancy is non-local – the permutable
let
-bindings may be
miles away inside the term. It requires a global approach to recover
canonicity, which we discuss in
Section

4
(
4
Saturated focused
\lambda
-calculus
)
.

3.2
Computational completeness

We can define a
depolarization
operation
\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}\right\rfloor_{\pm}
that
takes polarized types and erases polarity information. The definition
is given in full in
Definition
(
Definition

(
Depolarization
)
.
)
, but for example we have

\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}\right\rfloor_{\pm}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
,

\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}\right\rfloor_{\pm}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\right\rfloor_{\pm}\to\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\right\rfloor_{\pm}}}
,
and
\left\lfloor\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\right\rangle^{+}\right\rfloor_{\pm}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\right\rfloor_{\pm}
.

This erasure operation can be extended to a
defocusing

operation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}
on focused terms that preserves typing modulo depolarization. For
example, if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
holds, then
in the un-focused system

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\right\rfloor_{\pm},\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\right\rfloor_{\pm}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}}}:(\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\right\rfloor_{\pm}\mid\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}\right\rfloor_{\pm})}}

holds – with
(A\mid\emptyset)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}A
and
conversely. This operation is defined on terms as a direct mapping on
all
\lambda
-term formers, except the
let
-definition form
which does not exist in the unfocused calculus and is substituted
away:

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}/}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}
.

Going from a focused system to a system with less restrictions is
easy. The more interesting statement is the converse, that for any
un-focused term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
there exists an equivalent focused term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
.

Theorem 7

(Completeness of focusing)
.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\right\rfloor_{\pm},\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\right\rfloor_{\pm}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:(\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\right\rfloor_{\pm}\mid\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}\right\rfloor_{\pm})}}\\
\implies\par\exists{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}},\hskip 18.49988pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\hskip 9.24994pt\wedge\hskip 9.24994pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

Proof.

Completeness of focusing is a non-trivial result, but it is
independent to the contributions of the current work, and in
particular extends gracefully to the presence of an empty type. See
for example proofs in
Liang and Miller 2007
;
Ahmad et al. 2010
;
Simmons 2011
.
∎

3.3
Choices of polarization

We mentioned that a given un-polarized atom
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
must either appear
always positively
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
or always negatively
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}
in our
judgments. Violating this restriction would break completeness, as for
example
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}
is not provable – they are considered
distinct atoms. But the global choice of polarization of each atom is
completely free: completeness holds whatever choice is made. Those
choices influence the operational behavior of proof search:

Chaudhuri et al. 2008b
shows that using the
negative polarization for all atoms corresponds to backward proof
search, whereas using the positive polarization corresponds to forward
proof search.

Similarly, there is some leeway in insertion of the polarity shifts

\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\right\rangle^{+}
and
\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\right\rangle^{-}
; for example,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}}

and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}+\left\langle{\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}\right\rangle^{-}}\right\rangle^{+}}}
depolarize to the same
formula, but admit fairly different terms – the double-shifting
allows an invertible phase to start right after
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}})}}
. When
transforming a non-polarized type into a polarized type, two
strategies for inserting shifts are notable. One is to insert as few
shifts as possible; the terms inhabiting the minimally-shifted
judgment are in one-to-one correspondence with terms of the un-focused
system that “respect the focusing restriction”. The other is to
insert double-shifts under each connective; the terms inhabiting these
double-shifted judgments are in one-to-one correspondence with
unfocused sequent terms –
Zeilberger 2013
relates this to
double-negation translations from classical to intuitionistic logic.

4
Saturated focused
\lambda
-calculus

In
Section

3.1
(
3.1
(Non-)canonicity
)
we explained that the essential
source of non-canonicity in focused term systems is that distinct
non-invertible phases may be independent from each other: reordering
them gives syntactically distinct terms that are observably equivalent
in a pure calculus. Being such a reordering of another term is
a highly global property, that cannot be decided locally like
invertible commuting conversions.

Logicians introduced
maximal
multi-focusing

[
Chaudhuri et al. 2008a
]
to quotient
over those reorderings, and
Scherer and Rémy 2015
expressed this in a
programming setting as
saturation
. The idea of maximal
multi-focusing is to force each non-invertible phase to happen

as early as possible
in a term, in parallel, removing the
potential for reordering them. However, in general there is no
goal-directed proof search (or term enumeration) procedure that
generates only maximally multi-focused derivations, as one cannot
guess in advance what non-invertible phases will be useful in the rest
of the term – to introduce them as early as possible. Saturation is
a technique specific to intuitionistic logic: when a non-invertible
phase starts, instead of trying to guess which non-invertible phases
would be useful later, one saturates the context by performing

all
the possible left-focused phases,
let
-binding all
the neutrals that might be used in the rest of the term. One can think
of a neutral of positive type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}})}}
as an

observation
of the current environment: we are saturating by
performing all possible observations before making a choice – a right
focusing phase. Note this strategy would be invalid in an effectful
language, or a resource-aware logic where introducing unused
sub-derivations can consume necessary resources and get you stuck.

In general there may be infinitely many distinct observations that can
be made in a saturation phase – consider the context

({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}\to\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}\right\rangle^{-}}}}})
, and a type system
that would enforce complete saturation would then have to admit
infinite terms. Instead,
Scherer and Rémy 2015
relax the definition of
saturation by allowing saturation phases to introduce only a finite
subset of deducible neutrals
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}})}}
. They prove that
canonicity holds (in a system without the empty type) in the following
sense: if two saturated terms made the same saturation choices, then
they are equivalent if and only if they are syntactically the same –
modulo
(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}})
. In their work, the notion of equivalence
is
{\beta\eta}
-equivalence of the defocused forms.

4.1
The saturated type system

The saturated type system is given in

Figure

5
(
5Cut-free saturated focused type system
)
. The neutral judgments are
identical to the focused type system of

Figure

4
(
4Cut-free focused

λ

-terms
)
, and most of the invertible
rules are also identical. The only change is that the rule

sinv-sat
moving from the invertible phase to the focused phase,
instead of merging the two contexts
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
in a single
context position as
foclc-inv-foc
, now keeps them separate.
