\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{s\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid\emptyset}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\right\rangle^{+}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\\
\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{1}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{2}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{i}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{i}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{i}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{i}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{1}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{2}}}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}

This second position
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
represents the fragment of the context
that is
new
during the following saturation phase. The
saturation rule
sat
requires that any introduced neutral
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}

use at least one variable of this new context
(
\exists{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}},\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
). This guarantees that
a single neutral term cannot be introduced twice by distinct
saturation phases: the second time it will not be new anymore.

This new context is also used to know when saturation stops: if an
instance of the
sat
rule does not introduce any new neutral,
then on the next saturation phase the new context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
will be the
empty context
\emptyset
, allowing saturation to proceed to prove the
goal with one of the two other choice-of-focusing rules.

This aspect of the saturation judgment is reused, unchanged, from

Scherer and Rémy 2015
. On the other hand, the formulation of the
saturation rule
sat
is different. We pass the (potentially infinite)
set
S
of new introducible neutrals to a selection function

\mathtt{Select}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}}({S})
, which returns a finite subset of
neutrals to introduce in a given context.

\mathtt{Select}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}}({S})
may not return any subset, we give the
requirement for a selection function to be valid in

Section

4.3
(
4.3
Selection function
)
.

The notation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}
denotes simultaneous
binding of a (finite) set of neutral terms – our notion of syntactic

\alpha
-equivalence is considered to test the (decidable) set
equality.

4.2
Strong positive neutrals

To understand and formalize saturation it is interesting to compare
and contrast the various notions of deductions (seeing our type
systems as logics) at play; how to prove
A
in a context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}
?

•

The more general notion of deduction is the unfocused notion of
proof
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}A}}
– proof terms have no restriction. In
the focused system, it would correspond to looking for a proof of
an invertible judgment
\emptyset;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}A\mid\emptyset
.

•

The neutral judgments
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
correspond to a less expressive notion of
“simple deduction step”, which are iterated by saturation. For
example,
\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}}}}\right\rangle^{-}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}}
does

not
hold, it requires more complex reasoning than a chain of
eliminations from the context variables. Focusing decomposes
a non-focused reasoning into a sequence of such simple deduction
steps, separated by invertible phases of blind proof search.

One notion that is missing is the notion of what is “already known by
the context”. With the usual non-focused logic, to know if a positive
formula
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
has been introduced before, we simply check if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}

is in the context. But the focusing discipline decomposes positive
formulas and removes them from the context.

One could use the judgment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
instead –

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}^{+}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}^{+}}}
as intended.
But
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
is too strong for the purpose of just
retrieving information from the context, as it calls the general
invertible judgment at the end of the focused phase.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\right\rangle^{+}
holds whenever
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
is provable
from
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
, not only when
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
is an hypothesis in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
.

To capture this idea of what can be “retrieved” from the context
without any reasoning, we introduce in
Figure

6
(
6Strong positive judgment

Γ
na

⊢
p
⇛
P

)
the

strong
positive judgment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}
.

Figure 6:
Strong positive judgment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}

\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\right\rangle^{+}}}}\par\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{i}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{i}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{1}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{2}}}}}}

Strong positive neutrals correspond to the positive patterns of

Zeilberger 2009
. Those patterns describe the spine of
a non-invertible phase, but they can also characterize invertible
phases: an invertible phase, presented in a higher-order style,
provides a derivation of the goal for any possible positive pattern
passed by the environment. The two following results witness this relation.

Lemma 3

(Strong decomposition of invertible phases)
.

Consider an invertible derivation

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{s\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
: it starts with invertible
rules, until we reach a (possibly empty) “frontier” of saturated
subterms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}
to which the rule
sinv-sav
is applied. Let

\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{sat}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{a}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\right)^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k\in K}}}

be the family of such subterms. Then the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}
are exactly
the contexts such that

\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}},\par\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}},\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}

Lemma 4

(Strong positive cut)
.

If both
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{s\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:\emptyset\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
hold, then there exists
a subterm
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}
of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{foc}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
holds.

This result would not be provable for the more expressive judgment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
: there is no obvious way to substitute
a general
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}
through
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
that would respect the
focusing structure – and return a strict subterm.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}\implies{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
is true
but non-trivial, it relies on the compleness result – identity
expansion.

4.3
Selection function

Contrarily to the simpler setting with sums but no empty type, not
all ways to select neutrals for saturation preserve canonicity in
presence of the empty type. Consider for example the focused terms

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{(}}}))^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}}}\par{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{(}}}))^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}}}

at the typing

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\right\rangle^{+}\to\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}\right\rangle^{-}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}g}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\right\rangle^{+}\to\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\right\rangle^{-}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}}
.
The set of potential observations is

\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}g}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}\}
, and both terms made
the same choice of observing only
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}
. The first
term always returns
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}
and the second
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}
, so
they are syntactically distinct even modulo
(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}})
. Yet
they are
{\beta\eta}
-equivalent as the context is inconsistent. Note
that if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}g}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}
had been introduced
during saturation, the immediately following invertible phase would
necessarily have been
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{absurd}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}})}}
, and the two terms would thus be
syntactially equal.

To make saturation canonical again, we need a provability completeness
requirement: if there is a possible proof of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
, we want
saturation to find it. One could cheat, knowing that provability of
any formula is decidable in propositional logic, and test explicitly
for the absence of proof of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
; but saturation is already doing
proof search
2
2

2

Saturation synthetizes new sub-terms and can thus
decide equivalence with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
, unlike previous methods that
would only reorder the subterms of the compared terms.
, and
we can extend it gracefully to have this property.

We define our requirement on the selection function in

Figure

7
(
7Specification of saturation selection functions
)
. We require that, for any type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}

that is part of the deducible observations
S
(by a neutral

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}:\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-})}}
), either
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
is already retrievable
from the context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
(no need to introduce it then) or it is the
type of a neutral
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n^{\prime}}}
selected by the function. We do not require
the same neutral
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
to be selected: there may be infinitely many
different neutrals deducible at
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
, but just having one of them in
the returned set suffices. This definition is not natural, it will be
validated by the results from
Section

5
(
5
Saturation consistency
)
.

Note that the types
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
that can be simply deduced from the context

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
are subformulas of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
. We know by the
subformula property that they are also subformulas of the root
judgment of the global derivation. In particular, there is only
a finite number of such deducible types
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
– this would not hold
in a second-order type system. Valid selection functions exist thanks
to this finiteness.

Figure 7:
Specification of saturation selection functions

\frac{\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},S,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}},\hskip 9.24994pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}:\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}}}\in S\hskip 9.24994pt\implies\hskip 9.24994pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}\;\vee\;\exists({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n^{\prime}}}}}:\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}}}),\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n^{\prime}}}\in\mathtt{Select}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}}({S})}{\mathtt{Select}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}})\text{ is a valid selection function}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{select-specif}

Note that two valid selection functions can be merged into a valid
selection function, by taking the union of their outputs.

4.4
Completeness of saturation

Completeness of saturation is relative to a specific choice of
selection function. Indeed, consider the context

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\par\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\par({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\right\rangle^{+}\to\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}\right\rangle^{-}}}}})

In this context, the only deducible positive formula is

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
, and it is already retrievable from
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
. This means
that a selection function that would satisfy

\mathtt{Select}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}}({S})=\emptyset
would be a valid selection
function. However, saturating with such a selection function is not
computationally complete: the saturated term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}}
has
a valid derivation, but
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}}}
does
not – nor does any equivalent term.

We can order selection functions by pointwise subset ordering:
a function
f
is above
g
if it selects at least all of
g
’s
neutrals for each context. A set of saturation functions is
upward-closed if, for any saturation function in the set, any function
above it is in the set.

Theorem 8

(Completeness of saturation)
.

For any focused term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
, there
exists an upward-closed set of selections functions such that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{s\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
holds, for a (computable)
saturated term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
such that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}
.

Note that, given
two
focused term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}}}
,
we can merge the selection functions used in the theorem above to
get a single selection function for which there exists saturated
terms for both
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}}}
.

5
Saturation consistency

In this section, we prove the main result of this extension of
saturation to the empty type: if a context is inconsistent, then the
saturation phase will eventually introduce a variable of the empty
type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
in the context. This is key to obtaining a canonicity
result – if saturation sometimes missed proofs of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
, it could
continue with distinct neutral terms and result in distinct but
equivalent saturated terms.

The informal view of the different ways to deduce a positive formula
presented in
Section

4.2
(
4.2
Strong positive neutrals
)
(general proof,
simple deduction, retrieval from context) gives a specification of
what saturation is doing. From a high-level or big-step point of view,
saturation is trying all possible new simple deductions iteratively,
until all the positives deducible from the context have been added to
it. The following characterization is more fine-grained, as it
describes the state of an intermediary saturation judgment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{sat}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
.

The characterization is as follows: any formula that can be “simply
deduced” from the old context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
becomes “retrievable” in the
larger context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
. This gives a precise meaning to the
intuition that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
is “old”. What we mean when saying that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
is “new” can be deduced negatively: it is the part of the
context that is still fresh, its deductions are not stored in the
knowledge base yet.

Theorem 9

(Saturation)
.

If a saturated proof starts from a judgment of the form

\emptyset;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}_{0}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{sat}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

or

\emptyset;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}_{0}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{s\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

then for any sub-derivation of the form

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{sat}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

we have the following property:

\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}},\par{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}\par\implies\par{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}

Definition 11
.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
is
saturated
if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}

implies
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}
.

Corollary 2

(Saturation)
.

If a saturated proof starts from a judgment of the form

\emptyset;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}_{0}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{sat}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

or

\emptyset;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}_{0}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{s\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

then for any sub-derivation of the form

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{sat}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

the environment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
is saturated.

Lemma 5

(Saturated consistency)
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
is saturated, then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nvdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}
.

Theorem 10

(Inconsistent canonicity)
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}
, then for any
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}^{\prime}}}
such that

\emptyset;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{sat}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}\mathpunct{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}^{\prime}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
we have

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}^{\prime}}}
.

6
Canonicity

In this section we establish the main result of this article. If two
saturated terms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{s\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathpunct{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

are not syntactically equivalent (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
), then there
exists a model
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
in which a context distinguishes
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
from

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
: they are not contextually equivalent.

(We build distinguishing contexts in the un-focused

\lambda
-calculus, so technically we are distinguishing the defocused
forms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}
; the proof crucially relies on
the saturated structure of its inputs, but the code we generate for
computation and separation is more easily expressed unfocused.)

6.1
Sketch of the proof

Intuition

It is helpful to first get some intuition of what a pair of
syntactically distinct normal forms looks like, and what the
corresponding distinguishing context will look like. Suppose we have

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{s\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
. We can
explore
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
simultaneously, until we find the source of
their inequality.

The source of inequality cannot be in an invertible phase, given that
the term formers in invertible phase are completely determined by the
typing (modulo invertible commuting conversions); for example, if

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
is
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{1}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{2}}}
, we know that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
is of the form

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}\right)}}
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{2}\right)}}
, with

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}
for some
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}
– so we can continue
exploring
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}
. Same thing if the term
starts with a sum elimination (modulo
(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}})
one can
assume that they eliminate the same variable),

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}

: the subterms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}
in at least one of the
two branches differ.

Similarly, the source of inequality cannot be in the saturation phase,
where both terms saturate on neutrals that are completely determined
by the typing context – and the saturation selection function – they
are both of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}
for the
same set of neutrals on each side. The end of this saturation phase is
also type-directed, so both terms stop saturating (they get an empty

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
context) at the same time. The difference must then be in
the neutrals used in the
sat-up
or
sat-down
rules,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n^{\prime}}}
or
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}
. Note that we
cannot get a positive neutral on one side and a negative neutral on
the other, as usage of those rules is directed by whether the goal
type is a negative atom
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}
or a positive type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
.

Now, two neutrals
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n^{\prime}}}
or
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}

may differ because their
spine
differ, or because their
sub-terms that are outside the non-invertible phase differ. In the
latter case, finding the source of inequality is a matter of
traversing the common structure towards the sub-terms that differ. The
former case is more interesting – this pair of neutrals with distinct
spines is what we call
source of inequality
.

In the positive neutral case, we end up on either
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}}
with
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\neq{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}
, or distinct variables

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
of atomic type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
. In the negative neutral case,
one may encounter distinct neutrals with distinct structure, for
example
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}}}
at the same
type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
; negative neutrals should be looked “upside down”, as in
System L
[
Curien et al. 2016
]
: either their head variables differ, or
the same variable is applied a different sequence of elimination
rules.

In the easy case where the source of inequality is a sum constructor

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}})}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}})}}
, obtaining
a distinguishing context looks relatively easy: we need a context

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}\right]}}
that corresponds to the term traversal we performed
to reach this source of inequality. For example, if we had

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}\right)}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{1},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{2}\right)}}
because
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{2}
,
the context fragment corresponding to this reasoning step would be

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{2}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}
. This is trickier in the sum-elimination case: if we have

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}})^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}}

then we need our context to instantiate the variable
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
with the
right value
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}
so that the branch we want is taken – the
one with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}
. This is easy if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
is
a formal variable introduced by a
\lambda
-abstraction: at the point
where our context needs to distinguish the two
\lambda
-abstraction

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}}
, we need to use an
application context of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}})}}}}
. But

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
may have been introduced by a left-focusing step

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}
; then we need
to instantiate the variables of the observation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}

just so

that we get the desired result
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}}
. When the source of
inequality is on negative neutrals with heads
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M}}
or positive variables
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
, we need
the distinguishing context to pass values in the same way to get to
this source of inequality, and also to instantiate the variables

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
to get an inequality. If those variables are at an atomic
type, we must pick a model that replaces this atomic type by a closed
type, to instantiate them by distinguishable values at this closed
type.

Positive simplification

In order to simplify the following arguments, we will suppose that the
context and types of the two saturated proof to distinguish do not use
negative atoms, only positive atoms. In other words, the results only
holds for the focused system in the fragment

{\mathsf{\Lambda{}C}{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to}}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}},\mathord{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}})}}
.

This is a perfectly reasonable simplification in view of our goal,
which is to distinguish inequivalent non-focused term that have
distinct saturated normal forms: we know that any choice of
polarization for the non-focused atoms preserves the existence of
normal forms, so we can make them all positives – see

Section

3.3
(
3.3
Choices of polarization
)
.

In particular, the source of inequality (distinct neutrals whose spine
differs) is always a pair of neutrals
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}
, who
contain a syntactic difference
(
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}}
with
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\neq{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}
, or

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
) before the end of the non-invertible
phase. Negative neutrals are only used during saturation.

Neutral model

If
S
is a finite set, let us write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Fin}\left(S\right)}}
for the type

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\dots+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}}
that is in bijection with
S
(same
number of elements), witnessed by embeddings

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}})}}:S\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Fin}\left(S\right)}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{out}_{\mathtt{Fin}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}})}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Fin}\left(S\right)}}\to S
such that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{out}_{\mathtt{Fin}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}(x)}})}}}=x\in S\hskip 3.87498pt\wedge\hskip 3.87498pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{out}_{\mathtt{Fin}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}})}}})}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Fin}\left(S\right)}}}}

For any two distinct elements
x\neq y\in S
there exists
a distinguishing context for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}(x)}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}(y)}}
.

Definition 12

(Neutral model)
.

Given two syntactically distinct saturated terms

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

(with positive atoms only) with a source of inequality of the form

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}

we define the

neutral model

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}}}}
(or just
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}
)
by

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}_{0}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}^{+})\hskip 3.87498pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\hskip 3.87498pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Fin}\left(\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}^{+})}}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\}\right)}}

We say that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}})
contains a
code
for each atomic
variable bound at the source of inequality.

Distinguishing outline

The general idea of our distinguishing context construction is to
instantiate variables just so that each variable of atomic type

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+})}}
evaluates to its code
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})}}
. Thus, when we
end up on distinct neutrals
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}
, we know that our
context will send them to distinguishables values.

There are two moments where building a distinguishing context requires
synthesizing a closed value of a type: to instantiate the open
variables in the context of the two terms, and when distinguishing
invertible terms at a function type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}
, which we know to
be of the shape
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}})}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}})}}}}}
.

Synthesizing a value for a variable of atomic type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+})}}

is obvious, we just pick
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})}}
– this guarantees that, under
this context,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
will reduce to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})}}
as expected. For
a variable of sum type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}}
, we have to choose the value
to make sure that the correct branch of the two terms (the one
containing the source of inequality) will be explored, as previously
explained. For a variable
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
of negative type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}
, we have to
make sure that any observation of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
, any neutral term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}

whose head variable is
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
(in the syntax of
Curien et al. 2016

they are the
\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\mid S\rangle
), will reduce to the value we need: if
we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~\dots}}
, the neutral
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}

should reduce to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}})}}
. In other words, much in the spirit of
higher-order focusing
Zeilberger 2009
, we specify the
instantiation of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}})}}
by a mapping over all the
observations over
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
that are made in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}
.

Example

Consider for example:

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}})}}\to\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}\right\rangle^{-}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\begin{array}[]{c}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\begin{array}[]{l}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~z~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}})}}}}}}~\mathtt{in}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~o~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{z}}})}}}}}}~\mathtt{in}~z}}\end{array}}}\\
\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\begin{array}[]{l}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~z~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}})}}}}}}~\mathtt{in}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~o~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{z}}})}}}}}}~\mathtt{in}~o}}\end{array}}}\end{array}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}}

The shared context in this example is

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~z~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~o~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{z}}})}}}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}}}

and the source of inequality is
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}o}}
. The atomic
variables in the context at this point are
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}o}}
, so
we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+})={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Fin}\left(\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}o}}\}\right)}}
. We have to
provide a value for the negative variable
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
; its
“observations”, the arguments it is called with, are
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}

and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{z}}}
, so we define it on these inputs following the general
scheme:

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{n}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}&\mapsto&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}})}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}})}}}}}&\mapsto&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}o}})}}\\
\end{array}\right.

The value of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{n}}}
on the last element of

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}})
, namely

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}o}})}})}}}
, is not specified; the return type is
inhabited so a value can be chosen, and the specific choice does not
matter.

It is easy to check that the context

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}\right]}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{n}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}})}}\;\hat{n}}}}}

is such that plugging both terms will result in distinct closed
values of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+})
, namely

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}})}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}o}})}}
.
From there, building a distinguishing context returning a boolean is
trivial.

Technical challenge

We outlined the general argument and demonstrated it on an
example. Unfortunately, we found that scaling it to a rigorous,
general proof is very challenging.

When we define instantiation choices for negative types as mappings
from observations to their results, we implicitly rely on the fact
that the observations are distinct from each other. This is obvious
when the domain of these observations is made of first-order datatypes
(no functions), but delicate when some of those observations have
function types – consider observations against

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}}\right\rangle^{+}\to\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}\right\rangle^{-}}})}}
.

The natural idea is to inductively invoke the distinguishability
result: if

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}})}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}})}}}}
,
then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
are distinguishable and
\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
can
distinguish those two arguments by passing the right instantiation for

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
. However, making this intuition precise gets us into a quagmire
of self-references: to define instantiation of the variable
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
, we
may need to instantiate any such variable
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
whose scope it
dominates, but the argument for distinguishability is really on the
values that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}}
have reduced to by the
time they are passed to
\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
; but the natural way to denote
those values makes a reference to the instances passed for all the
variables in their scope,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
included…

We are convinced that there is a general inductive argument to be
found that would play in a beautiful way with general polarized type
structure. We have not found it yet, and shall for now use (a focused
version of) the function-removal hack from
Section

2.5
(
2.5
Fun-less types and reification
)
.

6.2
Saturated inequivalence

We argued that if we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
, then those terms
must be of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}\right]}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n^{\prime}}}}}\right]}}
or

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\right]}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}\right]}}
where the neutrals

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n^{\prime}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}
or
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}

have distinct
spines
(invertible phases), that is, they differ
even when ignoring their invertible sub-terms. With the simplifying
assumption that all atoms are positively polarized, only the case

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}
may arise.

This structure is crucial in the proof of canonicity, so we introduce
in
Figure

8
(
8Saturated inequivalence judgment
)
a precise inductive definition of this
decomposition as a
saturated inequivalence
judgment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi^{\prime}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}}}\right]}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

which represents constructive evidence of the fact that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\right]}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}\right]}}
, where
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}
are
the source of inequality as witnessed by the new judgment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}
.

The new structure
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}
is a
constraint environment
, a list of
equalities of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
or
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}

that correspond to knowledge that was accumulated during the
traversal of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}
: under a binding
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}
we remember

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
, and when doing a case-split on a variable

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{1}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{2}}}
we remember which branch leads to the source of
inequality by
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{i}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}^{\prime}}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
. Together,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi^{\prime}}}}}

form a
constrained environment
as used in

Fiore and Simpson 1999
. Note that when decomposing the variable

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{1}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{2}}}}}
we mention it in the constraint
environment, so we also keep it in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}
for scoping purposes: we use
general contexts with types of any polarity, not just negative or
atomic contexts
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
.

Two side-conditions in this judgment capture the essential properties
of saturated terms required to obtain canonicity. In the saturation
case, the condition
\forall{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\in\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}},\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\notin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}

enforces that
let
-bindings in the constraint environment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}
all bind distinct neutrals. At the source of inequality, the
condition
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nvdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}
enforces that the context is
consistent.
