Appendix B
Proof outlines

Theorem

3
(
Theorem 3

(
{\beta\eta}
is semantically sound)
.
)
.

This is proved by direct induction on the evidence that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
; semantic equivalence is a congruence, and

\beta
-reduction and
\eta
-expansions are identities in the semantic
domain.
∎

Theorem

4
(
Theorem 4

(Semantic equivalence implies contextual equivalence)
.
)
.

For any model
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
and context

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}\right]}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}}
, the
closed term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}})}}\right]}}
must have a normal form

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}
by
Lemma

1
(
Lemma 1

(Inversion)
.
)
, and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}})}}\right]}}
a normal form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}
. The
semantic interpretation of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\right]}}
(technically,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}})
for the natural semantics of
contexts with a hole) and of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}\right]}}
are equal by our
assumption
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{sem}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
and the fact that semantic
equality is a congruence. They are also are respectively equal to

({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}},\star)
and
({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}},\star)
by

Theorem

3
(
Theorem 3

(
{\beta\eta}
is semantically sound)
.
)
, so we must have
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}={{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}
.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}\right]}}
is not separating.
∎

Theorem

5
(
Theorem 5

(Reification)
.
)
.

We define

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}(v)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lceil{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify^{\prime}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lfloor{v}\rfloor_{A}}})}}}\rceil_{A}}}
using an (obvious) auxiliary function
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify^{\prime}}(v)}}
on
values at fun-less types, on which the inversion property is
immediate; for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}})}}
it is then proved using the fact
that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\rfloor_{A}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lceil{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\rceil_{A}}}

commute.
∎

Lemma

2
.

By
Theorem

3
(
Theorem 3

(
{\beta\eta}
is semantically sound)
.
)
, we can without loss of
generality consider
\beta
-normal forms only. For the fun-less

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify^{\prime}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}})}}
, direct induction proves

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify^{\prime}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}})}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
on normal-forms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
–
note that the fact that our types have no functions is key to
preserve the inductive invariant that our terms are closed,
necessary to use the inversion lemma to reason on the shape of

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
. The result for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}})}}
holds by commutation of

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\rfloor_{A}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lceil{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}\rceil_{A}}}
.
∎

Theorem

6
(
Theorem 6

(Contextual equivalence implies semantic equivalence)
.
)
.

We prove the contrapositive: if

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
in some model
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
, we
build a discriminating context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}\right]}}
in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
. Our
induction on a type
A
has a slightly stronger hypothesis. We
assume
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathpunct{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}:A({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}})}}

and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}
: the semantically
distinct terms are well-typed at the closed typing

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}(A)}}
, but need not be
well-typed at
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathord{\mathunderscore\!\mathunderscore}}}}}:A}}
.

In the function case
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A\to B}}
we have an input

w\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{A}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
where the two semantics differ, we build
the discriminating context

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}\;({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reify}_{A}(w)}})}})}}}}\right]}}
,
where
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}
distinguishes at
B
by induction hypothesis.

In the sum case,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
is of the form
({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}},v)
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}}
of the form
({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}},v^{\prime})
and we have either

{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\neq{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}
or
v\neq v^{\prime}
. In the second case, suppose for
example
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}={{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}=1
, we use a context

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}~\mathtt{with}~}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1}}}\right]}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{2}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{2}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}}}}}}\right)}}
, using the fact that the return type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}

is inhabited to build a dummy value for the unused branch
(
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\in\{1,2\}
is arbitrary).
∎

Lemma

4
(
Lemma 4

(Strong positive cut)
.
)
.

By simultaneous induction on
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
. In the variable case,
it’s just a variable/variable substitution.
∎

Theorem

8
(
Theorem 8

(Completeness of saturation)
.
)
.

The proof of
Scherer and Rémy 2015
can be reused almost as-is. It
defines a rewriting relation from focused terms to saturated terms,
that proceeds by moving
let
-bindings around: at any saturation
point in some context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
, it looks for all the negative
neutral terms in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
that would be well-typed in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
,
and saturates over exactly those neutral terms.

However, just selecting the neutral sub-terms of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
does not make
a valid selection function. We also have to add enough neutrals at
each saturation step to respect the validity criterion; by the
subformula property, a finite number of neutrals always suffices,
so there exists a valid selection function with the neutral
sub-terms of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
.

Finally, any selection function above this one also satisfies
completeness. Introducing more neutrals either does not change the
term shape (after the next invertible phase) or exposes a neutral
proof of inconsistency
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}})}}
; in the latter case,
more terms become
{\beta\eta}
-equivalent, which helps us proving our
goal
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}
.
∎

Theorem

9
(
Theorem 9

(Saturation)
.
)
.

By induction on the derivation. In the base case, the old context is
empty so the result is trivial – there is no
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
such that

\emptyset\mathrel{\Downarrow}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}
. The induction case considers
the application of the
sat
rule to deduce

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{sat}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}
. We have to prove that any sub-derivation of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
of the
form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{sat}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}

satisfies the expected property for any
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
, this is immediate by induction
hypothesis. If we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
but not

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
is only provable by a “new”
neutral
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
using variables from
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
, so it is passed to the
selection function. By validity of the selection function
(
Figure

7
(
7Specification of saturation selection functions
)
) we know that some neutral

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n^{\prime}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}
must then selected, so
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
occurs in the
positive context of the invertible term
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
. By

Lemma

3
(
Lemma 3

(Strong decomposition of invertible phases)
.
)
we thus have

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}
as required.
∎

Lemma

5
(
Lemma 5

(Saturated consistency)
.
)
.

By (logical) completeness of focusing it suffices to prove that
there is no
focused
proof of

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}}}}:\emptyset\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
.

By inversion on the possible derivations, we see that the invertible
phase must be empty, and
foclc-let-pos
is the only possible
choice of focusing:
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
is of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}
with

\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}\right\rangle^{-}\hskip 9.24994pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}}:\emptyset\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{foc}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{let}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathord{=}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}~\mathtt{in}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}
is saturated so
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}\right\rangle^{-}
implies

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\Rrightarrow$}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}}}
. But then, by
Lemma

4
(
Lemma 4

(Strong positive cut)
.
)
,
we know that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
has a subterm
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{foc}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
.

We have proved that any term in the judgment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{foc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
has a strictly smaller subterm itself in
the judgment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{foc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
. There is no such proof term.
∎

Theorem

10
(
Theorem 10

(Inconsistent canonicity)
.
)
.

The inversible and saturation phase are purely type-directed, so

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}}^{\prime}}}
necessarily use the same term-formers during
those phases. They could only differ on a neutral term, but they
cannot contain a derivation of the form

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{-}
or

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
as, by

Corollary

2
(
Corollary 2

(Saturation)
.
)
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
would be saturated,
which is incompatible with our assumption
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}

and
Lemma

5
(
Lemma 5

(Saturated consistency)
.
)
.
∎

Lemma

6
(
Lemma 6

(Saturated inequivalence)
.
)
.

By direct induction on
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
. In particular, the
two side-conditions we mentioned are direct consequences of the
saturated term structure.

The fact that the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
bindings are never redundant is
a direct consequence of the “new”-ness condition on saturated
neutrals:
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
cannot be added in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}
in a future phase as it
would not be new anymore.

The fact that the context is consistent at the source of inequality
is a consequence of
Corollary

2
and

Lemma

5
(
Lemma 5

(Saturated consistency)
.
)
.
∎

Theorem

11
(
Theorem 11

(Inequivalence in the model)
.
)
.

We prove this by simultaneous induction on the inequivalence evidence
for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}_{0}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}_{0}}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}_{0}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}}}\right]}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

and the sub-terms of the two terms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}
, building
inequivalence evidence for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}
. There are
three interesting cases: when the current term-former introduces
a variable
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+})}}
in context (or at the root for the
variables of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}
), the saturation step (where
non-redundancy is checked), and when the source of inequality

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}
is finally encountered.

When we enter a binding
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}}
, the corresponding subterms
of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}
start with a large pattern-matching on the
possible values of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+})}}
. We decide
to take the branch corresponding to the case
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})}}
,
maintaining the inductive invariant that the sub-terms of

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}
are equal to the sub-terms of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}
under
the family of substitutions

\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}/}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}\right)^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}
, for all variables

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
of atomic type introduced so far.

In a saturation step, we must check that the newly added bindings
are not already present in the constrained environment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}
. The neutrals introduced in

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}
are of the form

{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}/}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
, and this substitution
preserves
\alpha
-inequivalence, as it replaces distinct variables
by distinct values.

When we reach the source of inequality

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}
, the derivation of spine
inequivalence
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}
either ends up
on a
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}

with
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\neq{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}
, in which case the corresponding neutral
subterms of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}
are also of this form, or

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}}
. In this case the subterms of

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}
are
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}})}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{in}_{\mathtt{Fin}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}})}}
, by our inductive
invariant: they are also distinct positive neutrals, and form
a source of inequality.

At the source of inequality, we also have to prove that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nvdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}
from the
hypothesis
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nvdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}
. Note that this could be wrong
if we applied some other model
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}}}
; for example, the context

({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}^{+}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}}})
is consistent, but
applying the model
\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}}^{+}\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\}

results in an inconsistent context.

Fortunately, the definition of the neutral model

Definition

12
(
Definition 12

(Neutral model)
.
)
is such that the positive atoms that
become inhabited are exactly those that have bound variables in the
context at the source of inequality

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
. And, by saturated
consistency (
Corollary

2
(
Corollary 2

(Saturation)
.
)
), we know that those positive
atoms are exactly those that are
provable
in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
.

We can thus can prove

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nvdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}
by contradiction
as follows. Suppose we have a proof of

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}
. Any subproof of
the form

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+})}}
,
for one of the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
inhabited in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}
, can be replaced by
a subproof of the form

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}}})
, as we know that all
such
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
are derivable in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}
. The transformed proof does
not rely on the definition of the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
, so it is also a valid proof
of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}}
, which is impossible.
∎

Corollary

3
(
Corollary 3

(Fun-elimination preserves semantic equivalence)
.
)
.

This is immediate from
Lemma

8
and the fact
that the function-elimination transformation is a bijection on
semantics value –
Figure

3
(
3Fun-less data types
)
.
∎

Theorem

12
(
Theorem 12

(Canonicity)
.
)
.

We remark that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{sem}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}

is a trivial property if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}
is a closed type (no atoms
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}^{+}
):
by inspecting the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}\mathrel{\Uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}
judgment (or
the strong positive neutral structure), we know that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}
differ on a constructor, and this property is preserved by
any substitution.

From
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nvdash}}}\emptyset}}
we know that for any
A\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}
,

A
is inhabited by some closed values –

Corollary

1
(
Corollary 1

(Inhabited or inconsistent)
.
)
. We can always pick an
arbitrary closed value of type
A
.

We build
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}
by peeling off the variables of the environment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}
, from the most recently introduced to the first introduced. At
any point during our induction, we have processed a fragment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}}^{\prime}}}

of the context, and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}}
remains unprocessed, with

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}=({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}}^{\prime}}})
, and we have a substitution
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}
that binds
the variables of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}}^{\prime}}}
to closed terms, such that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}
contains no inconsistent
equation. Initially we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta}}^{\prime}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\emptyset
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\emptyset
.

When
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}
is of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A_{1}+A_{2}}}}}
, we look
for equations of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}
; by
validity of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}
(
Definition

13
), there is at
most one. If there is none, we choose an arbitrary value for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}

in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}
; if there is one, we choose

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}
; this is a closed term as
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}

must be defined after
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
in the context.

When
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}
is of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}}
, we look for
equations the family of equations

\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}\right]}}\right)^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i\in I}}}}
– remember that we write

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}\right]}}
when the neutral
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
has head
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
. Let us
write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}\right]}}
for this set of equations about
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
.

We define the value for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}
as

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\right)^{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i\in I}}}})}}
where

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}})}}
, defined below, is such that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}})}}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}
always holds, if
all equations in
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}
are of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}\right]}}
.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}})}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}()}}\par{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{1}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{2}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}})}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}{\!\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{1}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{1}\mid\{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{1}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}}\right]}})\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}\})}}\!\!}\\
,\\
{\!\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{2}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{2}\mid\{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{2}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}}\right]}})\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}\})}}\!\!}\\
\end{array}\right)}}\par{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}:\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}\mid\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}\})}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}\par{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}:\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}\mid\emptyset)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}\text{arbitrary}

From the validity of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}
, we know that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}\right]}}
contains
at most one equation of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}})}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}
for each
possible neutral
\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}}}}\mathrel{\Downarrow}\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}
, which justifies
the only two cases in the definition of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}}:\left\langle{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}\right\rangle^{-}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}})}}
above.
∎

Corollary

4
(
Corollary 4

(Canonicity)
.
)
.

We use a stronger induction hypothesis on the inequivalence
judgment: if

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi^{\prime}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}}}\right]}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

then there is a substitution
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}}:({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}})
and
a context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}\right]}}
such as

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}}}\right]}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\beta}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}}}\right]}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}}}}

where
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\right]}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}\right]}}
.

In the base case
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}
, we get a substitution

{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}}:({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi^{\prime}}}}})
from
Theorem

12
(
Theorem 12

(Canonicity)
.
)
;
the theorem also gives us

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{sem}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}
, so there
is a distinguishing context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}
as required as contextual and
semantic equivalence coincide.

In the sum-elimination case

\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{1}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{2}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi^{\prime}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}^{\prime}}}}}}}\right]}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{1}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}_{2}}}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\begin{array}[]{l}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{match}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}~\mathtt{with}~}}\\
\left|\begin{array}[]{l}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j}}}\neq{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\end{array}\right.\end{array}}}}\!\!\!\!\right)}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi^{\prime}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}^{\prime}}}}}}}\right]}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}}

the substitution
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}
verifies, by inductive hypothesis, that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}\;{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}
, so in
particular the sum-elimination term, once applied
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}
, will
reduce to
({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\right]}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}
and

({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}\right]}}){{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}}
respectively, as desired: this
means that the distinguishing context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}
for the premise is also
a distinguishing context for the sum-elimination terms.

In the (left) pair case

\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi^{\prime}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}}}\right]}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{1}\mid\emptyset}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}\right)}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi^{\prime}}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}}}\right]}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{1}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}_{2}}}\mid\emptyset}

We can obviously keep the same
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}
, and build the
distinguishing context
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{1}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}}}\right]}}
, where
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}
is
the distinguishing context for the inductive premise.

From this stronger inductive hypothesis
({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}})
, we can
build a distinguishing context for

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}}}\right]}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}}}\right]}}
as

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\lambda}{({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1}\dots{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{n}\in{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}})}.\,{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\square}}}}})}}\;({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}})\dots({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}_{n}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}}})}}}}\right]}}

∎

Theorem

13
(
Theorem 13

(Saturated terms are canonical)
.
)
.

Let
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
.

By
Lemma

6
(
Lemma 6

(Saturated inequivalence)
.
)
we have

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}}\mid\emptyset}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}_{0}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\mathsf{na}}^{\prime}}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathtt{ne}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}_{0}}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}_{0}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}}}}\right]}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}}

with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}_{0}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}_{0}}}}}\right]}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}_{0}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}_{0}}}}}\right]}}
, that is,

({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leadsto}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}_{0}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}_{0}}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}_{0}}}}}}}\right]}}
.

We consider the neutral model
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}}}}
defined in

Definition

12
(
Definition 12

(Neutral model)
.
)
. Let
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}})
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}})
.

By
Theorem

11
(
Theorem 11

(Inequivalence in the model)
.
)
we have

({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leadsto}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}_{1}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}_{1}}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}_{1}}}}}}}\right]}}
,
that is a inequivalence of terms at the atom-less judgments

\emptyset;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathtt{p}}}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash_{\mathsf{inv}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}})\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}_{\mathsf{a}}}})

.

Using the focused function-elimination transformation of

Figure

9
(
9Fun-less focused forms
)
, let us define

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}}}\rfloor}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{2}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{def}}}{{=}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}}\rfloor}}
.
By
Lemma

7
(
Lemma 7

(Function elimination preserves inequivalence)
.
)
we get

({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{2}}})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leadsto}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}_{2}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}_{2}}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}_{2}}}}}}}\right]}}
,
that is a saturated inequivalence at closed types without functions.

From
Corollary

4
(
Corollary 4

(Canonicity)
.
)
, we finally get

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}_{2}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}_{2}}}}}\right]}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D}}_{2}}}\left[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}^{\prime}}}_{2}}}}}\right]}}
,
that is
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{2}}}
, a contextual inequivalence at
closed types without functions. As contextual and semantic
equivalence coincide at closed types, this is equivalent to

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{2}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{2}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}
,
that is,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}}}\rfloor}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lfloor{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}}\rfloor}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}
.

By
Corollary

3
(
Corollary 3

(Fun-elimination preserves semantic equivalence)
.
)
, we thus have that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{1}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{1}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}
at closed types,
that is,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}}\,\right\rrbracket}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}}
, that
is,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{sem}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}})}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}_{0}}}
, that is,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{sem}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}
, or equivalently

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}_{0}}}
.
∎

Corollary

5
(
Corollary 5

(Contextual equivalence implies equality of saturated forms)
.
)
.

By contrapositive, we show that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}^{\prime}}}
implies

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
. If we had
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}^{\prime}}}
we would
have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}^{\prime}}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}
by

Theorem

13
(
Theorem 13

(Saturated terms are canonical)
.
)
. As
{\beta\eta}
-equivalence
implies contextual equivalence, we have

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}^{\prime}}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}
, so
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cancel{\approx}_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
by
transitivity.
∎

Corollary

6
(
Corollary 6
.
)
.

We know from
Section

2
(
2
Equivalences in the full
\lambda
-calculus
)
that
{\beta\eta}
-equivalence
implies contextual equivalence. Conversely, we suppose

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{ctx}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
and prove
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
.

By
Theorem

7
(
Theorem 7

(Completeness of focusing)
.
)
, there exists focused forms

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}^{\prime}}}
that erase to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
(respectively) modulo

{\beta\eta}
; we can even pick them in the subset with positive atoms
only.

By
Theorem

8
(
Theorem 8

(Completeness of saturation)
.
)
, there exists saturated forms

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}}^{\prime}}}
that have the same erasures as
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}^{\prime}}}

(respectively) modulo
{\beta\eta}
, that is they erase to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}

modulo
{\beta\eta}
.

By
Corollary

5
(
Corollary 5

(Contextual equivalence implies equality of saturated forms)
.
)
, we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}}^{\prime}}}
. As the invertible commuting conversions are sound with
respect with
{\beta\eta}
-equality, we have

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lfloor{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}}^{\prime}}}}}\right\rfloor_{\mathtt{foc}}}}
, that is,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{{\beta\eta}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}^{\prime}}}
.
∎

Corollary

7
(
Corollary 7
.
)
.

To test if two terms are equivalent, we invoke the focusing
completeness result then the saturated completeness result, and
check if the normal form coincide modulo
(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}})
. Those
two transformations are computable and
(\mathord{\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\approx_{\mathtt{icc}}}}}})
is
decidable, so equivalence is decidable.
∎

Corollary

8
(
Corollary 8
.
)
.

Our notion of semantic equivalence coincides with
{\beta\eta}
and
contextual equivalence, and only distinguishes terms using sets
isomorphic to closed types, which are all finitely inhabited.
∎
